c^{\prime}
such that if
\Delta^{2}\geq c_{\gamma}\left(\log(n)+\sqrt{\frac{pK\log(n)}{n}}\right)
, the following holds with probability at least
1-\frac{c^{\prime}}{n^{2}}
. For all
i\in I_{1}
,
\hat{k}_{i}=k^{*}_{i}
.

Then, on the high probability event on which Lemma
G.5
holds and on which the clustering procedure from
[
49
]
onto the projected dataset
\hat{p}(Y^{(2)})
recovers exactly the restriction of
G^{*}
to
I_{2}
, Linear Discriminant Analysis with the centers
\mu_{k}^{(2)}
also does perfect classification. We are then able to recover the entire partition
G^{*}
. This concludes the proof of the corollary.

∎

G.1.2
Hierarchical Clustering

In this Section, we apply a single linkage hierarchical clustering procedure on the dataset
\hat{p}\left(Y^{(2)}\right)
. We consider the case where
p\geq\frac{n}{K}
.
Let
\hat{G}
be the projected hierarchical clustering procedure obtained from Algorithm
2
. For any two disjoint sets
A,B
we define the single linkage function
l(A,B)=\min_{i\in A,i^{\prime}\in B}\|Y_{i}-Y_{i^{\prime}}\|
.

Data:

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}

Draw
(\delta_{i})_{i\in[1,n]}
independently and uniformly on
\{1,2\}
;

Compute
\hat{v_{1}},\ldots,\hat{v}_{K}
the leading eigenvectors of
\left(Y^{(1)}\right)^{T}Y^{(1)}-nI_{p}
, with
Y^{(1)}
the restriction of
Y
to
I_{1}
;

For
i\in I_{2}
, compute
\hat{p}(Y_{i})
the orthogonal projection of
Y_{i}
on the space
Vect\left(\hat{v_{1}},\ldots,\hat{v}_{K}\right)
(if
p\leq\max\left(\log(n),K\right)
keep
Y_{i}
);

t\leftarrow 0
;

G^{(0)}\leftarrow\left\{\{i\}_{i\in I_{2}}\right\}
;

while

t<|I_{2}|-K

do

Find
\hat{a},\hat{b}
minimizing
l\left(G^{(t)}_{\hat{a}},G^{(t)}_{\hat{b}}\right)
;

Build
G^{(t+1)}
by merging the groups
G^{(t)}_{\hat{a}}
and
G^{(t)}_{\hat{b}}
, the other groups remaining unchanged;

t\leftarrow t+1
;

end while

\hat{G}:=\hat{G}^{(t)}
;

for

k\in[1,K]

do

Compute
\hat{\mu}_{k}:=\frac{1}{|\hat{G}_{k}|}\sum_{i\in\hat{G}_{k}}X_{i}
end for

for

i\in I_{1}

do

Assign
i
to the group
\hat{G}_{k}
minimizing
\|X_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{k}\|
. end for

Result:

The partition
\hat{G}
.

Algorithm 2

Hierarchical Clustering algorithm with single linkage after splitting and projecting the dataset

The following proposition provides separation conditions under which Algorithm
2
perfectly recover the partition
G^{*}
with high probability.

Proposition G.6
.

There numerical constants
c
and
c^{\prime}
and a positive constant
c_{\gamma}
that only depends on
\gamma
such that the following holds. Suppose
n\geq p\geq\frac{n}{K}
,
n\geq cK^{2}
, and

\Delta^{2}\geq c_{\gamma}\left(\log(n)+\sqrt{\frac{pK^{2}\log(n)}{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Denoting
\hat{G}
the output of Algorithm
2
, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}[\hat{G}=G^{*}]\geq 1-c^{\prime}/n^{2}
.

If
p\leq\max\left(\log(n),K\right)
, we have that
\hat{p}
is the identity and we can therefore assume that
p\geq\max\left(\log(n),K\right)
.

Conditionally on
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
, the dataset
Y^{(2)}
is independent from
Y^{(1)}
and thus
Y^{(2)}
is independent from
\hat{p}
. We deduce from this that
\hat{p}(Y^{(2)})
is a Gaussian mixture of dimension
\max\left(\log(n),K\right)
which is well separated with high probability (Lemma
G.1
provides a separation at least
\Delta^{2}/4
). With high probability, using Lemma
G.2
, we also have
|I_{2}|\geq n/4
and for all
k\in[K]

|I_{2}\cap G^{*}_{k}|\geq\frac{n}{4K\gamma}
.

Hence, applying a Hierarchical procedure ensures that, if
\Delta^{2}\geq c^{\prime\prime}\left(\log(n)+\sqrt{K\log(n)}\right)
–see Proposition 4 in
[
28
]
, then we recover exactly with high probability the restriction of the partition
G^{*}
to
I_{2}
. We write the obtained partition
\hat{G}^{(2)}
. Note that the condition
\Delta^{2}\geq c^{\prime\prime}\left(\log(n)+\sqrt{K\log(n)}\right)
is ensured if the constant
c_{\gamma}
in the statement of the proposition such that
\Delta^{2}\geq c_{\gamma}[\log(n)+\sqrt{\frac{pK^{2}\log(n)}{n}}]
is large enough. Hence,
I_{1}
is perfectly clustered. In turn, we deduce that
I_{2}
is perfectly clustered by applying by arguing as in the previous proof.

∎

The proof of Proposition
G.6
also porvides a result when we do not make any assumption on the dimension. We state this result as a proposition.

Proposition G.7
.

There numerical constants
c
and
c^{\prime}
and a positive constant
c_{\gamma}
that only depends on
\gamma
such that the following holds. Suppose
n\geq cK^{2}
, and

\Delta^{2}\geq c_{\gamma}\left(\log(n)+\sqrt{K\log(n)}+\sqrt{\frac{pK^{2}\log(n)}{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Denoting
\hat{G}
the output of Algorithm
2
, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}[\hat{G}=G^{*}]\geq 1-c^{\prime}/n^{2}
.

G.1.3
Proof of Lemma
G.1

We work conditionally on
I_{1}
. To ease the notation, we write
Y=Y^{(1)}
and
n^{\prime}=|I_{1}|
. Without loss of generality, we restrict ourselves in the following to the event where
n^{\prime}\geq n/4
and for all
k\in[K]
,
|I_{1}\cap G_{k}^{*}|\geq|G_{k}^{*}|/4\geq\frac{n}{4\gamma K}
(Lemma
G.2
). We remark that
\hat{v}_{1},\ldots,\hat{v}_{K}
are also the
K
leading eigenvectors of
Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}
. We seek to find an event of high probability on which

•

the quantity
x^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x
is uniformly large for unit vectors such that
|\langle x,\frac{\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}}{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|}\rangle|
is large enough, for some
k\neq l
,

•

the
(k+1)
-th eigenvalue
\hat{\lambda}_{k+1}
of
Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}
is small.

Such an event is provided by Lemma
G.8
and
G.9
, respectively proven in Section
G.1.3
and
G.1.3
.

Lemma G.8
.

There exists a positive constant
c_{\gamma}
that only depends on
\gamma
such that, if
\Delta^{4}\geq c_{\gamma}\frac{pK^{2}}{n}
, the following holds with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{n^{2}}
. Simultaneously on all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\|x\|=1
and such that there exists
k\neq l
with
|\langle x,\frac{\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}}{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|}\rangle|\geq\frac{1}{2}
, we have

x^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x\geq\frac{n}{256\gamma K}\Delta^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma G.9
.

There exists a positive constant
c_{\gamma}
that only depends on
\gamma
such that, if
\Delta^{4}\geq c_{\gamma}\frac{pK^{2}}{n}
, the following holds with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{n^{2}}
. Simultaneously on all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\|x\|=1
and such that
x\in\left(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}\right)^{\perp}
, we have

x^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x\leq\frac{n}{512\gamma K}\Delta^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

In the following, we suppose
\Delta^{4}\geq c_{1}\frac{pK^{2}}{n}
, with
c_{1}
a numerical constant large enough such that Lemma
G.8
and Lemma
G.9
both hold. We restrict ourselves to the event of probability at least
1-\frac{4}{n^{2}}
defined as the union of the two events of Lemma
G.8
and Lemma
G.9
.

Lemma
G.9
implies that the
(k+1)
-th largest eigenvalue of
\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)
satisfies
\hat{\lambda}_{k+1}\leq\frac{n}{512\gamma K}\Delta^{2}
. Let
k\neq l
and
y=\frac{\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}}{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|}
. We decompose
y=\hat{p}(y)+(y-\hat{p}(y))
. Since
\langle y,y\rangle=1
, then either
\langle y,\hat{p}(y)\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2}
, either
\langle y,(y-\hat{p}(y))\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

Let us suppose that
\langle y,(y-\hat{p}(y))\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2}
and let us find a contradiction. Using Lemma
G.8
, we deduce that
(y-\hat{p}(y))^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)(y-\hat{p}(y))\geq\frac{n}{256\gamma K}\Delta^{2}
. However,
y-\hat{p}(y)
is the orthogonal projection of
y
onto the space spread by the eigenvectors corresponding to the eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{k+1},\ldots,\hat{\lambda}_{p}
. Thus,
(y-\hat{p}(y))^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)(y-\hat{p}(y))\leq\hat{\lambda}_{k+1}\leq\frac{n}{512\gamma K}\Delta^{2}
, which leads to a contradiction.

Thus,
\langle y,\hat{p}(y)\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Hence,
\|\hat{p}(\mu_{k})-\hat{p}(\mu_{l})\|^{2}=\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|^{2}\|\hat{p}(y)\|^{2}=\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|^{2}\langle y,\hat{p}(y)\rangle^{2}\geq\frac{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|^{2}}{4}
. This concludes the proof of the lemma.

In the proof of this lemma, we write
A\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n^{\prime}\times K}
for the assignment matrix defined by
A_{ik}=\mathbf{1}_{i\in G^{*}_{k}}
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times p}
for the matrix of the means whose
k
-th row is
\mu_{k}
, and
E\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}\times p}
the noise matrix
Y-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Y]
which is distributed as i.i.d. standard normal distributions.

Using the decomposition
Y=A\mu+E
, we have
x^{T}(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p})x=x^{T}(E^{T}E-n^{\prime}I_{p})x+2x^{T}(A\mu)^{T}Ex+x^{T}(A\mu)^{T}(A\mu)x
. The three following lemmas, proved in Sections
G.1.3
,
G.1.3
and
G.1.3
, control each of these terms. Let us define the set of suibable unit vectors

\mathcal{X}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\|x\|=1,\text{ and}\,\exists k\neq l\in[K]\kern 5.0pts.t\kern 5.0pt\langle x,\frac{\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}}{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|}\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma G.10
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

x^{T}(A\mu)^{T}(A\mu)x\geq\frac{n}{64\gamma K}\Delta^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma G.11
.

With probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, simultaneously on all unit vectors
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we have

|x^{T}\left(EE^{T}-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x|\leq 4\sqrt{n^{\prime}(6p+4\log(n))}+48p+32\log(n)\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma G.12
.

With probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, simultaneously on all unit vectors
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we have

|x^{T}(A\mu)^{T}Ex|\leq\frac{1}{4}\|A\mu x\|^{2}+4\left(\sqrt{K}+7\sqrt{p+2\log(n)}\right)^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Combining Lemmas
G.10
,
G.11
, and
G.12
, we deduce that, with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{n^{2}}
, simultaneously on all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\displaystyle x^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|A\mu x\|^{2}-8\left(\sqrt{K}+7\sqrt{p+2\log(n)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle-4\sqrt{n^{\prime}(6p+4\log(n))}-48p-32\log(n)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{n}{128\gamma K}\Delta^{2}-8\left(\sqrt{K}+7\sqrt{p+2\log(n)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle-4\sqrt{n^{\prime}(6p+4\log(n))}-48p-32\log(n)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{n}{128\gamma K}\Delta^{2}-c^{\prime}\left(K+p+\sqrt{pn}+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

with
c^{\prime}
a numerical constant. Let us now restrict ourself to the event of probability
1-\frac{2}{n^{2}}
on which, simultaneously on all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the above inequality is true. We recall the hypothesis
n\geq p\geq\max\left(K,\log(n)\right)
. Under this hypothesis,
\left(K+p+\sqrt{pn}+\sqrt{n\log(n)}\right)\leq 4\sqrt{pn}
. Thus, if the constant
c_{\gamma}
such that
\Delta^{4}\geq c\frac{pK^{2}}{n}
is large enough, we conclude conclude that.

x^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x\geq\frac{n}{256\gamma K}\Delta^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

It remains to prove Lemmas
G.10
,
G.11
, and
G.12
.

∎

Let us take a unit vector
x
such that, for some
k\neq l
, we have
|\langle x,\frac{\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}}{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|}\rangle|\geq\frac{1}{2}
. We write
y=\frac{\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}}{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|}
and we compute

\displaystyle x^{T}(A\mu)^{T}(A\mu)x=

\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}\in[1,K]}\sum_{a\in G_{k^{\prime}}^{*}}\langle\mu_{k^{\prime}},x\rangle^{2}\geq\frac{n}{4K\gamma}\left(\langle x,\mu_{k}\rangle^{2}+\langle x,\mu_{l}\rangle^{2}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Using the hypothesis
|\langle x,y\rangle|\geq\frac{1}{2}
, we deduce that
|\langle x,\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\rangle|\geq\frac{\Delta}{2}
and therefore
|\langle x,\mu_{k}\rangle|\geq\frac{\Delta}{4}
or
|\langle x,\mu_{l}\rangle|\geq\frac{\Delta}{4}
. Hence,
x^{T}(A\mu)^{T}(A\mu)x\geq\frac{n}{64\gamma K}\Delta^{2}
. This concludes the proof of the lemma.

∎

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\|x\|=1
, we have

|x^{T}(E^{T}E-n^{\prime}I_{p})x|\leq\|EE^{T}-n^{\prime}I_{p}\|_{op}\kern 5.0pt.

We use the next lemma for upper-bounding this quantity. We refer for example to the textbook
[
34
]
(Lemma 12.10, page 273).

Lemma G.13
.

There exists a random variable
\xi
with exponential distribution of parameter
1
such that

\|E^{T}E-n^{\prime}I_{p}\|_{op}\leq 4\sqrt{n^{\prime}(6p+2\xi)}+48p+16\xi\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, we have

\|E^{T}E-n^{\prime}I_{p}\|_{op}\leq 4\sqrt{n^{\prime}(6p+4\log(n))}+48p+32\log(n)\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof of the lemma.

∎

We denote
P
the orthogonal projection onto the rows of
A\mu
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\|x\|=1
, we have

\displaystyle|x^{T}(A\mu)^{T}Ex|=

\displaystyle|\langle A\mu x,Ex\rangle|=|\langle A\mu x,PEx\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|A\mu x\|\|PEx\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|A\mu x\|\|PE\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\|A\mu x\|^{2}+4\|PE\|_{op}^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The next lemma, which is just proved below, provides an upper-bound of the quantity
\|PE\|_{op}
.

Lemma G.14
.

With probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, we have

\|PE\|_{op}\leq\left(\sqrt{K}+7\sqrt{p+2\log(n)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma
G.14
implies that, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, simultaneously on all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
\|x\|=1
, we have

x^{T}(A\mu)^{T}Ex\leq\frac{1}{4}\|A\mu x\|^{2}+4\left(\sqrt{K}+7\sqrt{K+2\log(n)}\right)^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

This lemma is stated as an exercise in
[
34
]
(exercise 12.9.6, page 288). Let
r\leq K
denote the rank of
A\mu
. We define
u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}
an orthogonal basis of the space spanned by the rows of
A\mu
. Let
U=\left(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}\right)
. Then
P=UU^{T}
. For any unit norm vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we have

\displaystyle x^{T}(PE)^{T}(PE)x=

\displaystyle x^{T}E^{T}P^{2}Ex

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{T}E^{T}PEx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{T}E^{T}UU^{T}Ex

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{T}(U^{T}E)^{T}(U^{T}E)x\kern 5.0pt.

Thus,
\|PE\|_{op}=\|U^{T}E\|_{op}
. Moreover, the columns of
U^{T}E
are independent with law
\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{r}\right)
. Let us now take again some
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
\|x\|=1
. We denote
W=U^{T}E
and get

\displaystyle\|Wx\|^{2}=

\displaystyle x^{T}W^{T}Wx=x^{T}(W^{T}W-KI_{n})x+K

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K+\|W^{T}W-KI_{n}\|_{op}\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, we have
\|W\|^{2}_{op}\leq K+\|W^{T}W-KI_{p}\|_{op}
. Applying Lemma
G.13
, we deduce the existence of an exponential random variable
\xi^{\prime}
such that
|W|_{op}^{2}\leq\left(\sqrt{K}+7\sqrt{p+\xi^{\prime}}\right)^{2}
. Hence, with probability
1-\frac{1}{n^{4}}
, we have

\|W\|_{op}\leq\sqrt{K}+7\sqrt{p+2\log(n)}\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof of the lemma.

∎

Let
x\in\left(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}\right)^{T}
be a unit vector. We have
A\mu x=0.
Thus, we have
x^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x=x^{T}\left(E^{T}E-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x
. In turn, we have

x^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x\leq\|EE^{T}-n^{\prime}I_{p}\|_{op}\kern 5.0pt.

By Lemma
G.13
, we have that, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
,

\|E^{T}E-n^{\prime}I_{p}\|_{op}\leq 4\sqrt{n^{\prime}(6p+4\log(n))}+48p+32\log(n)\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, uniformly on all unitary
x
,

x^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x\leq 4\sqrt{n^{\prime}(6p+4\log(n))}+48p+32\log(n)\kern 5.0pt,

Recall
n\geq p\geq\log(n)
. If the constant
c_{\gamma}
such that
\Delta^{4}\geq c_{\gamma}\frac{pK^{2}}{n}
is large enough, we have that, with the same high probability, uniformly on all such unit
vectors
x
,

x^{T}\left(Y^{T}Y-n^{\prime}I_{p}\right)x\leq\frac{n}{512\gamma K}\Delta^{2}\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

G.2
Proof of Proposition
4.3

Without loss of generality, we suppose that
\sigma^{2}=1
. Let
E^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
with i.i.d
\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{2})
entries. Then
Y^{(1)}=(Y+E^{\prime})/\sqrt{2}
and
Y^{(2)}=(Y-E^{\prime})/\sqrt{2}
are two independent datasets such that, when
i\in G_{k}^{*}
, we both have
Y^{(1)}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{k}/\sqrt{2},I_{p}\right)
and
Y^{(1)}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{k}/\sqrt{2},I_{p}\right)
. Our strategy follows the two steps;

1.

Use the first dataset
Y^{(1)}
in order to estimate the set
J^{*}
of active columns;

2.

Use a clustering procedure to the second dataset, keeping only columns estimated in the first step.

For the first step, we consider
\hat{J}
collecting the
s
columns of
Y^{(1)}
with the largest euclidean norm. We recall the definition

w_{J^{*}}:=\min_{j\in J^{*}}\sum_{i\in[n]}X_{ij}^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Next lemma states that, if
w_{J^{*}}
is large enough, then
\hat{J}
contains
J^{*}
with high probability.

Lemma G.15
.

There exists a numerical constant
c_{1}>0
such that the following holds. If

w^{2}_{J^{*}}:=\min_{j\in J^{*}}\sum_{i\in[n]}X_{ij}^{2}\geq c_{1}\left(\sqrt{n\log(pn)}+\log(p)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(78)

then, with probability higher than
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
,
\hat{J}
contains
J^{*}
.

Let us then work conditionally on
\hat{J}
and let us suppose that
\hat{J}
indeed contains
J^{*}
. Let
Y^{(2)}_{\hat{J}}
the restriction of
Y^{(2)}
to the columns
j\in\hat{J}
. Since
Y^{(2)}
is independent from
\hat{J}
, we deduce that
Y^{(2)}_{\hat{J}}
is a Gaussian Mixture with a separation
\Delta^{2}/2
in dimension
s
. We can conclude the proof using Proposition
3.2
. We deduce that, except when
s\in[\text{Poly-log}(n),n/K]
and
n\in[K^{2},K^{c}]
, with
c
some numerical constant, if

\Delta^{2}\overset{\log}{\geq}1+\min\left(\sqrt{s},\sqrt{\frac{sK^{2}}{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

then it is possible to recover exactly
G^{*}
with high probability and with an algorithm computable in polynomial time.

In order to prove that
\hat{J}
contains
J^{*}
with high probability, it is sufficient to prove that, with high probability, for all
j\in J^{*}
and for all
j^{\prime}\in[p]\setminus J^{*}
, we have
\|Y^{(1)}_{:j}\|^{2}>\|Y^{(1)}_{:j^{\prime}}\|^{2}
.
We decompose the matrix
Y^{(1)}=X/\sqrt{2}+E
with
E
a gaussian matrix. For
j\in[p]
, we have

\|Y^{(1)}_{:j}\|^{2}=\frac{\|X_{:j}\|^{2}}{2}+\|E_{:j}\|^{2}+\sqrt{2}\langle X_{:j},E_{:j}\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

Using Hanson-Wright inequality inequality for Gaussian variables (e.g. Lemma 1
[
46
]
) and the tail of a gaussian random variable, we deduce that, with probability higher than
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, uniformly on all
j\in[p]
, we have

\left|\|Y^{(1)}_{:j}\|^{2}-n-\frac{1}{2}\|X_{:j}\|^{2}\right|\leq c^{\prime}\left(\sqrt{n\left(\log(pn)\right)}+\|X_{:j}\|\sqrt{\log(pn)}+\log(pn)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

(79)

for some
c^{\prime}>0
. On this same event of high probability, we have that, for
j\in[p]
for which
X_{:j}=0
,

\|Y^{(1)}_{:j}\|^{2}\leq n+c^{\prime}\left(\sqrt{n\left(\log(pn)\right)}+\log(pn)\right)\kern 5.0pt.

In light of (
79
), if we take the constant
c_{1}
large enough in (
78
), we conclude that

\min_{j\in J^{*}}\|Y_{:j}\|^{2}>\max_{j\notin J^{*}}\|Y_{:j^{\prime}}\|^{2}
, which concludes the proof of the lemma.

∎

G.3
Proof of Proposition
4.6

We suppose without loss of generality that
\sigma^{2}=1
. Let
E^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
with i.i.d
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries. Then
Y^{(1)}=(Y+E^{\prime})/\sqrt{2}
and
Y^{(2)}=(Y-E^{\prime})/\sqrt{2}
are two independent datasets such that, when
i\in G_{k}^{*}
, we both have
Y^{(1)}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{k}/\sqrt{2},I_{p}\right)
and
Y^{(1)}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{k}/\sqrt{2},I_{p}\right)
.

For any partition
G
of
[n]
into
K
groups, we denote
B^{G}
the associated normalized partnership matrix defined by

B^{G}_{ij}=\sum_{k\in[K]}\frac{1}{|G_{k}|}\mathbf{1}\left\{i\in G_{k}\right\}\mathbf{1}\left\{j\in G_{k}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

The application
G\to B^{G}
is a bijection from the set of all partitions into
K
groups to the set of matrices (Lemma 12.3 of
[
34
]
page 262)

\mathcal{B}=\left\{B\in S_{n}(\mathbb{R})^{+}:\kern 5.0ptB_{ij}\geq 0,Tr(B)=K,B1=1,B^{2}=B\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

To alleviate the notation, we write
B^{*}
for
B^{G^{*}}
.

For any such normalized partnership matrix
B\in\mathcal{B}
with associated partition
G
, we define
\hat{J}(B)
as the subset of the
s
indices
j\in[p]
that maximizes the square
l_{2}
norm

\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{a\in G_{k}}Y^{(1)}_{aj}\right)^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Then, for
B\in\mathcal{B}
and
J\subseteq[p]
, we define the criterion on
Y^{(2)}
.

Crit(B,J)(Y^{(2)})=\langle Y^{(2)}_{J}(Y^{(2)}_{J})^{T}-|J|I_{n},B\rangle\kern 5.0pt.

Take any two distinct
B,B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
. We define the partial ordering relation
{}^{\prime}\preceq^{\prime}
by
B\preceq B^{\prime}
if

Crit(B,\hat{J}(B)\cup\hat{J}(B^{\prime}))(Y^{(2)})\leq Crit(B^{\prime},\hat{J}(B)\cup\hat{J}(B^{\prime}))(Y^{(2)})\kern 5.0pt.

(80)

Finally, we define
\hat{B}
and the associated partition
\hat{G}
as any maximal
B
with respect to this ordering.

In fact, we will show that, provided that
\Delta^{2}
and
w_{J^{*}}
are large enough, we have, with high probability,
B\prec B^{*}
for all
B\in\mathcal{B}
, which in turn implies that
\hat{G}=G^{*}
.

Let us shortly discuss the definition of our estimator. Given
B
,
\hat{J}(B)
selects the
columns with empirical largest norms, ie those which are most likely to contain the informative columns. Then,
B\preceq B^{\prime}
corresponds to the fact that the Kmeans criterion restricted to the columns in
\hat{J}(B)\cup\hat{J}(B^{\prime})
is smaller for
B^{\prime}
than for
B
–see e.g.
[
62
]
for the connection between Kmeans criterion and
Crit
. Here, we use a simple sample splitting scheme to avoid technicalities in the simultaneous control of the
\hat{J}(B)
’s and of the Kmeans criterion.

The proofs proceeds with two main steps. First, by Lemma
4.2
,
\hat{J}(B^{*})
contains
J^{*}
with high probability as long as
w_{J^{*}}
is large enough.

Then, we work conditionally on the event of Lemma
4.2
. The property
J^{*}\subseteq\hat{J}(B^{*})
implies that, for any
B\in\mathcal{B}
,
Y^{(2)}_{\hat{J}(B)\cup\hat{J}(B^{*})}
is a gaussian mixture of dimension at most
2s
with a separation
\Delta^{2}/2
. The next lemma, which builds upon previous analyses of the exact Kmeans criterion
[
28
]
, states
B^{*}
is a global maximum of the restricted Kmeans criterion provided the separation is large enough.

Lemma G.16
.

Assume that
\Delta^{2}\geq c\gamma^{5/2}\left[\sqrt{\frac{sK}{n}\left[\log(n)\right]}+\log(n)\right]
where
c
is a large enough numerical constant and assume that
J\subseteq\hat{J}(B^{*})
. With probability at least
1-\frac{2}{n^{2}}
, we have

Crit(B,\hat{J}(B)\cup\hat{J}(B^{*}))(Y^{(2)})<Crit(B,\hat{J}(B^{*})\cup\hat{J}(B^{*}))(Y^{(2)})\kern 5.0pt,

simultaneously for all
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

Combining Lemmas
4.2
and Lemma
G.16
leads to the desired result.

G.3.1
Proof of Lemma
4.2

In this proof, we write
Y
instead of
Y^{(1)}
for simplicity of notation.
In order to prove that
J^{*}\subseteq\hat{J}(B^{*})
with high probability, we prove that, with high probability, uniformly on all
j\in J^{*}
and
j^{\prime}\notin J^{*}
,

\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}Y_{aj}\right)^{2}\geq\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}Y_{aj^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

For any
j\in[p]
, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}Y_{aj}\right)^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(|G_{k}^{*}|(\mu_{k})_{j}+\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}E_{aj}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|^{2}(\mu_{k})_{j}^{2}+\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}E_{aj}\right)^{2}+2\sum_{k\in[K]}(\mu_{k})_{j}\left(\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}E_{aj}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The quadratic noise term

\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}E_{aj}\right)^{2}=E_{:j}^{T}SE_{:j}
with
S
the
n\times n
matrix defined by
S_{ij}=\sum_{k}\mathbf{1}\left\{i,j\in G_{k}^{*}\right\}
. Thus, using Hanson-Wright Lemma (see Appendix B.6 of
[
34
]
for example), we deduce that, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, uniformly on all
j\in[p]
, we have,

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}E_{aj}\right)^{2}-\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|\right|\leq

\displaystyle c\sqrt{\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|^{2}\left(\log(n)+\log(p)\right)}+c\max_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|\left(\log(n)+\log(p)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{\prime}\gamma\left(\sqrt{\frac{n^{2}}{K}\left(\log(n)+\log(p)\right)}+\frac{n}{K}\left(\log(n)+\log(p)\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

for some numerical constants
c
and
c^{\prime}
.

The cross-product term

The random variable
2\sum_{k\in[K]}(\mu_{k})_{j}\left(\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}E_{aj}\right)
is normally distributed with variance
4\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|(\mu_{k})_{j}^{2}
. So, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, for all
j\in[p]
, and for some numerical constant
c
,

2\left|\sum_{k\in[K]}(\mu_{k})_{j}\left(\sum_{a\in G^{*}_{k}}E_{aj}\right)\right|\leq c\sqrt{\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|(\mu_{k})_{j}^{2}\left(\log(n)+\log(p)\right)}\kern 5.0pt,

for some constant
c>0
.

Let us restrict ourselves to an event of high probability on which those two deviation hold. If
j\notin J^{*}
, then, for some
c>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{a\in G_{k}}Y_{aj}\right)^{2}-\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|\leq

\displaystyle c\gamma\left(\sqrt{\frac{n^{2}}{K}\left(\log(n)+\log(p)\right)}+\frac{n}{K}\left(\log(n)+\log(p)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\gamma\frac{n}{K}\left(\log(np)+\sqrt{K\log(np)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\gamma^{2}\min_{k}|G_{k}^{*}|\left(\log(np)+\sqrt{K\log(np)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\min_{k}|G_{k}^{*}|w_{J^{*}}^{2}

provided the constant
c_{1}
such that
w_{J^{*}}^{2}\geq c_{1}\gamma^{2}(\sqrt{K\log(np)}+\log(np))
is large enough.

Let us turn to the case where
j\in J^{*}
. Provided that the numerical constant
c_{1}
in the condition
w_{J^{*}}^{2}\geq c_{1}\gamma^{2}\left(\sqrt{K\left(\log(np)\right)}+\log(np)\right)
is large enough, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{a\in G_{k}}Y_{aj}\right)^{2}-\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|\geq

\displaystyle\min|G_{k}^{*}|\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|(\mu_{k})_{j}^{2}-c\sqrt{\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|(\mu_{k})_{j}^{2}\log(np)}-\min|G_{k}^{*}|\frac{1}{8}w^{2}_{J^{*}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\min|G_{k}^{*}|w_{J^{*}}\kern 5.0pt.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.

G.3.2
Proof of Lemma
G.16

In this section, for the sake of simplicity, we write
Y
instead of
Y^{(2)}
and for any
B\in\mathcal{B}
, we write
Y_{B}
the restriction of
Y
to the columns in
\hat{J}(B^{*})\cup\hat{J}(B)
. We recall that we work conditionally on
(\hat{J}(B))_{B\in\mathcal{B}}
and that we suppose
J\subseteq\hat{J}(B^{*})
. We denote
s_{B}=|\hat{J}(B^{*})\cup\hat{J}(B)|\leq 2s
.

For
B\in\mathcal{B}
, we decompose the observations as

Y_{B}=X_{B}+E_{B}\kern 5.0pt,

where
(X_{B})_{ij}=\mu_{kj}
if
i\in G_{k}^{*}
and for
j\in\hat{J}(B^{*})\cup\hat{J}(B)
, and

E_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s_{B}}
is the restriction of
Y-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[Y]
to the columns
\hat{J}(B^{*})\cup\hat{J}(B)
.
Let us decompose the difference of the criterions.

\displaystyle Crit(B^{*},\hat{J}(B^{*})\cup\hat{J}(B))(Y)-Crit(B,\hat{J}(B^{*})\cup\hat{J}(B))(Y)

\displaystyle=\langle Y_{B}Y_{B}^{T}-s_{B}I_{n},B^{*}-B\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle X_{B}X_{B}^{T},B^{*}-B\rangle+\langle E_{B}E_{B}^{T}-s_{B}I_{n},B^{*}-B\rangle+2\langle X_{B}(E_{B})^{T},B^{*}-B\rangle=S(B)+N(B)+C(B)\kern 5.0pt.

As mentionned earlier in the proof, this corresponds to the difference of a Kmeans criterion
[
62
]
, which has been thoroughly studied in
[
28
]
.

In the remainder of this proof, we write
\|A\|_{1}
for its entry-wise
l_{1}
norm.
Using directly Lemma 4 from
[
35
]
, we deduce that the signal term satisfies

S(B)=\langle X_{B}X_{B}^{T},B^{*}-B\rangle\geq\frac{1}{4}\Delta^{2}\delta_{B}\kern 5.0pt,

(81)

with
\delta_{B}=\|B^{*}-B^{*}B\|_{1}
. It remains to upper-bound the quadratic noise term
\langle E_{B}E_{B}^{T}-s_{B}I_{n},B^{*}-B\rangle
and the crossed term
\langle X_{B}(E_{B})^{T},B^{*}-B\rangle
with respect to
\delta_{B}
. The next two lemmas provide an uniform control of these two terms.

Lemma G.17
.

There exists a numerical constant
c_{1}
such that the following holds. With probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, we have

|N(B)|\leq c_{1}[\delta_{B}\vee 1]\left[\sqrt{\frac{\gamma sK}{n}\left[\log(n)+\gamma^{2}\right]}+\log(n)+\gamma^{2}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

simultaneously over all
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma G.18
.

There exists a numerical constant
c_{2}
such that the following holds. With probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{2}}
, we have

|C(B)|\leq c_{2}\sqrt{S(B)(\delta_{B}\vee 1)\left(\log(n)+\gamma^{2}\right)}\kern 5.0pt,

simultaneously for all
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

We consider henceforth that we are under the event of probability higher than
1-2/n^{2}
where the deviation bounds in Lemma
G.17
and
G.18
hold. Then, for any
B\in\mathcal{B}\setminus\{B^{*}\}
, we have

\displaystyle S(B)-|N(B)|-|C(B)|\geq\frac{S(B)}{2}-(c_{1}+2c_{2})[\delta_{B}\vee 1]\left[\sqrt{\frac{\gamma sK}{n}\left[\log(n)+\gamma^{2}\right]}+\log(n)+\gamma\right]

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{B}\frac{\Delta^{2}}{8}-(c_{1}+2c_{2})[\delta_{B}\vee 1]\left[\sqrt{\frac{\gamma sK}{n}\left[\log(n)+\gamma^{2}\right]}+\log(n)+\gamma\right]\ .

Besides, we know from Lemma 9 in
[
28
]
that
\delta_{B}\geq m/(m)\geq 1/\gamma
if
B\neq B^{*}
. Hence,
[\delta_{B}\vee 1]\leq\gamma\delta_{B}
. We deduce that

S(B)-|N(B)|-|C(B)|\geq\delta_{B}\frac{\Delta^{2}}{8}-(c_{1}+2c_{2})\gamma\delta_{B}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\gamma sK}{n}\left[\log(n)+\gamma\right]}+\log(n)+\gamma\right]\ ,

This last quantity is positive provided that the constant
c
in the condition

\Delta^{2}\geq c\gamma^{5/2}\left[\sqrt{\frac{sK}{n}\left[\log(n)\right]}+\log(n)\right]

is large enough.

We have proved that
S(B)-|N(B)|-|C(B)|>0
for all
B\neq B^{*}
which, in light of the definition of
S(B)+N(B)+C(B)
, leads to the desired result.

Le us denote
m=\min_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|\geq\frac{n}{K\gamma}
. For a fixed
B
,
N(B)=\langle E_{B}E_{B}^{T}-s_{B}I_{n},B^{*}-B\rangle
is a quadratic form of Gaussian random variables. Thus, we are in position to apply Hanson-Wright inequality for Gaussian variables–see e.g. Lemma 1 in
[
46
]
. For a fixed
B\in\mathcal{B}
, with probability higher than
1-2e^{-x}
, we have

|N(B)|\leq c\left(\sqrt{s_{B}x\|B^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}}+x\|B^{*}-B\|_{op}\right)\kern 5.0pt\ ,

(82)

where
c
is a numerical constant. The next Lemma constrol
\|B^{*}-B\|_{F}
and
\|B^{*}-B\|_{op}

Lemma G.19
.

For all
B
, we have
\|B-B^{*}\|_{F}\leq 6\sqrt{\frac{\delta_{B}}{m}}
and

\|B-B^{*}\|_{op}\leq 2
.

We remark that the quantity
\delta_{B}
is upper-bounded by
2n
. We use a peeling-type argument/For
j\in[2n]
, we denote

\mathcal{B}_{j}:=\left\{B\in\mathcal{B},\kern 5.0pt\delta_{B}\in(j-1,j]\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

We shall apply the definition inequality (
82
) together with an union bound over
\mathcal{B}_{j}
, this for all
j=1,\ldots,2n
. The following lemma is a direct consequence of Lemma 17 in
[
28
]
.

Lemma G.20
.

There exists a positive numerical constant
c
such that, for any
j=1,\ldots,2n
, we have

\log\left[|\mathcal{B}_{j}|\right]\leq cj\left[\log(n)+\frac{m^{+}}{m}\right]\ ,

where
m^{+}=\max|G^{*}_{k}|
.

By definition, we have
m^{+}/m\leq\gamma
.
By Lemma
G.20
, we deduce that

\log(\mathcal{B}_{j})\leq cj[\log(n)+\gamma]
for some constant
c>0
.

Putting everything together we conclude that, with probability at leat
1-1/n^{2}
, we have

|N(B)|\leq c[\delta_{B}\vee 1]\left[\sqrt{\frac{\gamma sK}{n}\left[\log(n)+\gamma\right]}+\log(n)+\gamma\right]\ .

∎

The proof is based on standard linear algebra and follows from the computations in
[
35
]
and
[
28
]
. Since
B
and
B^{*}
are projector, we have
\|B-B^{*}\|_{op}\leq 2
. Besides, we have

B-B^{*}=(I-B^{*})(B-B^{*})(I-B^{*})+B^{*}(B-B^{*})+(B-B^{*})B^{*}+B^{*}(B-B^{*})B^{*}

Since
B^{*}
is a projector, we have
\|B^{*}(B-B^{*})B^{*}\|_{F}\leq\|(B-B^{*})B^{*}\|_{F}
. It follows that

\|B-B^{*}\|_{F}\leq\|(I-B^{*})(B-B^{*})(I-B^{*})\|_{F}+3\|(B-B^{*})B^{*}\|_{F}\ ,

(83)

In the proof of Lemma 13 in
[
28
]
, it is shown that

\|(I-B^{*})(B-B^{*})(I-B^{*})\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\delta_{B}}{m}}

Besides, it is shown in proof of Lemma 15 in
[
28
]
, that

\|(B-B^{*})B^{*}\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{2\frac{\delta_{B}}{m}}\ .

The result follows.
∎

Observe that the random variable
C(B)
is distributed as a Gaussian random variables whose variance is given by

4\|(B^{*}-B)X_{B}\|_{F}^{2}=tr[X_{B}^{T}X_{B}-X_{B}^{T}BX_{B}]=4S(B)\ ,

since
B^{2}=B
and
B^{*}X_{B}=X_{B}
.
Then, we apply an union bound over all
\mathcal{B}_{j}
’s and all
\mathcal{B}_{c,j^{\prime}}
. Together with Lemma
G.20
, this allows us to conclude that, with probability higher than
1-1/n^{2}
, we have

C(B)\leq c\sqrt{S(B)\lceil\delta_{B}\rceil\left(\log(n)+\frac{m^{+}}{m}\right)}\ ,

simultaneously for all
B\in\mathcal{B}
. Since
m_{+}/m\leq\gamma
, the result follows.

∎

G.4
Proof of Proposition
5.2

As in the proof of Proposition
4.6
, we express the exact Kmeans criterion in terms of partnership matrices.

Given a partition
G
, we define
B_{r}^{G}
s the corresponding partnership matrix, that is
B_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is such that
(B_{r})_{ij}=0
if
i
and
j
are not in the same group, whereas
(B_{r})_{i,j}
is equal to 1 over the size of group that contains
i
and
j
otherwise. For short, we write
B_{r}^{*}
for
B_{r}^{G^{*}}
. Also,
\mathcal{B}_{r}
for the collection of all possible partnership matrices of size
n
with
K
groups.

Simarly, we define partnership matrice
B_{c}^{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
associated to a partition
H
, the collection
\mathcal{B}_{c}
of all such partnership matrices, whereas we denote
B^{*}_{c}
for
B_{c}^{H^{*}}
. Finally, given the bi-Kmeans estimator
(\hat{G},\hat{H})
from (
46
), we write
\hat{B}_{c}
and
\hat{B}_{r}
for
\hat{B}_{c}^{\hat{G}}
and
\hat{B}_{r}^{\hat{H}}
.

We will often use that any
B_{r}\in\mathcal{B}_{c}
(resp.
B_{c}\in\mathcal{B}_{c}
) is a projection matrix and that its trace is equal to
K
(resp.
L
).

Partnership matrices are handy representations for analyzing Kmeans criteria
[
62
,
35
]
. Indeed, equiped with this notation, the bi-Kmeans estimator (
46
) can be reformulated as

\left(\hat{B}_{r},\hat{B}_{c}\right)=\arg\max_{B_{r}\in\mathcal{B}_{r},B_{c}\in\mathcal{B}_{c}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[Y^{T}B_{r}YB_{c}\right]\ .

(84)

Developing the criterion inside (
46
), we have that

(\hat{G},\hat{H})\in\mathop{\text{argmax}}_{G,H}\underset{l\in[L]}{\sum_{k\in[K]}}\ \underset{j\in H_{l}}{\sum_{i\in G_{k}}}2Y_{ij}\bar{Y}_{kl}^{G\times H}-\left(\bar{Y}_{kl}^{G\times H}\right)^{2}.

Then, we observe

\displaystyle\underset{j\in H_{l}}{\sum_{i\in G_{k}}}2Y_{ij}\bar{Y}_{kl}^{G\times H}-\left(\bar{Y}_{kl}^{G\times H}\right)=\underset{l\in[L]}{\sum_{k\in[K]}}|G_{k}||H_{l}|\left(\bar{Y}_{kl}^{G\times H}\right)^{2}=\|B_{r}YB_{c}\|_{F}^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[Y^{T}B_{r}YB_{c}\right]\ ,

since
B_{r}
and
B_{c}
are orthogonal projectors.
∎

In the following proposition, we write
m_{r}=\min_{a=k,\ldots,K}|G^{*}_{k}|
and
m^{+}_{r}=\max_{k=1,\ldots,K}|G^{*}_{k}|
, the respective size of the smallest group and of the largest group of the true partition of the rows. We similarly define
m_{c}
and
m_{c}^{+}
. By assumption, we have

\max(m_{r}^{+}/m_{r},m_{c}^{+}/m_{c})\leq\gamma
.

Throughout the proof of the proposition, we write
X=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[Y\right]
the signal matrix and
E=Y-X
the noise matrix, whose entries are i.i.d
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
. We suppose without loss of generality that
\sigma^{2}=1
. As for Proposition
4.6
, this proof heavily builds upon the analysis of the exact Kmeans criterion in
[
28
]
. By definition of our estimator, we have

\mathrm{Tr}\left[Y^{T}B_{r}YB_{c}\right]\geq\mathrm{Tr}\left[Y^{T}B^{*}_{r}YB^{*}_{c}\right]

The latter inequality implies that

S(\hat{B}_{r},\hat{B}_{c})\leq N(\hat{B}_{r},\hat{B}_{c})+C(\hat{B}_{r},\hat{B}_{c})

(85)

where

\displaystyle S(B_{r},B_{c})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[X^{T}B^{*}_{r}XB^{*}_{c}\right]-\mathrm{Tr}\left[X^{T}B_{r}XB_{c}\right]\ ;

(86)

\displaystyle N(B_{r},B_{c})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[E^{T}B_{r}EB_{c}\right]-\mathrm{Tr}\left[E^{T}B^{*}_{r}EB^{*}_{c}\right]\ ;

(87)

\displaystyle C(B_{r},B_{c})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle 2\mathrm{Tr}\left[X^{T}B_{r}EB_{c}\right]-2\mathrm{Tr}\left[X^{T}B^{*}_{r}EB^{*}_{c}\right]\ .

(88)

Here,
S(B_{r},B_{c})
is a deterministic signal term that only depends on
X
, whereas
N(B_{r},B_{c})
is a pure noise term that only depends on
E
. For
B_{r}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}
, we write
\delta_{B_{r}}=\|B_{r}^{*}-B_{r}^{*}B_{r}\|_{1}
. Similarly, for
B_{c}\in\mathcal{B}_{c}
, we write
\delta_{B_{c}}=\|B_{c}^{*}-B_{c}^{*}B_{c}\|_{1}
.

Lemma G.21
.

For any
B_{r}
and
B_{c}
, we have

S(B_{r},B_{c})\geq\left[\delta_{B_{c}}\frac{\Delta^{2}_{c}}{4}\right]\vee\left[\delta_{B_{r}}\frac{\Delta^{2}_{r}}{4}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma G.22
.

There exists numerical constants
c,c^{\prime}
such that, with probability higher than
1-c^{\prime}/(n\vee p)^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle N(B_{c},B_{r})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}\delta_{B_{r}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{p}{m_{r}m_{c}}\left[\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle+c\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\delta_{B_{c}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{n}{m_{r}m_{c}}\left[\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle+c\left[\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}\delta_{B_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\delta_{B_{c}}\right]\left[\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right]\ ,

simultaneously over all
B_{r}
and
B_{c}
.

Lemma G.23
.

There exists numerical constants
c,c^{\prime}
such that, with probability higher than
1-c^{\prime}/(n\vee p)^{2}
, we have

C(B_{c},B_{r})\leq c\sqrt{S(B_{r},B_{r})\left[\delta_{B_{r}}\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}\left(\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}\right)+\delta_{B_{c}}\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\left(\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right)\right]}\ .

simultaneously over all
B_{r}
and
B_{c}
.

We now use that
m^{+}_{r}/m_{r}\leq\gamma
and
m_{c}^{+}/m_{c}\leq\gamma
. By combining the two previous lemmas, we deduce that, for some numerical constants
c,c^{\prime}
, with probability at least
1-c^{\prime}/(n\vee p)^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle N(B_{c},B_{r})+C(B_{c},B_{r})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{S(B_{r},B_{r})}{2}+c\gamma^{5/2}\delta_{B_{r}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{KL\log(n\vee p)}{n}}+\log(n\vee p)\right]

\displaystyle+c\gamma^{5/2}\delta_{B_{c}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{KL\log(n\vee p)}{p}}+\log(n\vee p)\right]\ .

Let us specify this inequality to
\hat{B}_{c}
and
\hat{B}_{r}
. Coming back to (
85
) and using the lower bound of
S(\hat{B}_{c},\hat{B}_{c})
from Lemma
G.21
, we observe that, necessarily we have
\hat{B}_{c}=B_{c}^{*}
and
\hat{B}_{r}=B_{r}^{*}
. It remains to prove the lemmas.

By Linearity, we have

S(B_{r},B_{c})=S(B^{*}_{r},B_{c})+\mathrm{Tr}\left[X^{T}(B^{*}_{r}-B_{r})XB_{c}\right]=S(B^{*}_{r},B_{c})+\mathrm{Tr}\left[(XB_{c})(XB_{c})^{T}(B^{*}_{r}-B_{r})\right]\ ,

since
B_{c}
is a projector. Observe that the rows of
XB_{c}
are identical on each group of the true partition of the rows. Hence, it follows from Lemma 4 in
[
35
]
that
\mathrm{Tr}[(XB_{c})(XB_{c})^{T}(B^{*}_{r}-B_{r})]\geq 0
for any
B_{r}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}
. Hence, we have
S(B_{r},B_{c})\geq S(B^{*}_{r},B_{c})
. Then, we deduce again from Lemma 4 in
[
35
]
, that

S(B^{*}_{r},B_{c})\geq\frac{\Delta_{c}^{2}}{4}\delta_{B_{c}}\ .

The result of the lemma then follows by reversing the role of
B_{c}
and
B_{r}
.

∎

We first decompose
N(B_{r},B_{l})
into a sum of three terms
N(B)=N_{1}(B_{c})+N_{2}(B_{r})+N_{3}(B_{r},B_{c})
where

\displaystyle N_{1}(B_{c})=\mathrm{Tr}\left[E^{T}B_{r}^{*}E(B_{c}-B_{c}^{*})\right]

\displaystyle N_{2}(B_{r})=\mathrm{Tr}\left[E^{T}(B_{r}-B_{r}^{*})EB_{c}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle N_{3}(B_{r},B_{c})=\mathrm{Tr}\left[E^{T}(B_{r}-B_{r}^{*})E(B_{c}-B_{c}^{*})\right]\ .

Since
B_{r}^{*}
is a rank
K
projector, observe that
N_{1}(B_{c})
corresponds to the pure noise term in the analysis of a Kmeans criterion for a Gaussian mixture model in dimension
K
with
p
observations and
L
groups. Thus, we could apply Lemma 11 in
[
28
]
to control it. Similarly,
N_{2}(B_{r})
corresponds to the pure noise term in the analysis of a Kmeans criterion for a Gaussian mixture model in dimension
L
with
n
observations and
K
groups. Still, as the term
N_{3}(B_{r},B_{c})
is slightly more involved, we provide a dedicated proof.

For the simultaneous control of these three quantities, we will apply Hanson-Wright inequality together with a dedicated pealing argument. For any integer
j\in[1,2n]
, (resp.
j\in[1,2p]
), we define
\mathcal{B}_{r,j}=\{B\in\mathcal{B}_{r}:\delta_{B_{c}}\in(j-1,j]\}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}_{c,j}=\{B_{c}\in\mathcal{B}_{c}:\delta_{B_{c}}\in(j-1,j]\}
). Since
\delta_{B_{r}}
is always smaller than
2n
, this give us a partition of
\mathcal{B}_{r}\setminus\{B^{*}_{r}\}
. We shall apply Hanson-Wright inequality together with an union bound to each of these sets.

For this purpose, we need to control the Frobenius and operator norm of
B_{r}-B_{r}^{*}
and of
B_{c}-B_{c}^{*}
. Adapting Lemma
G.19
to our setting lead us to

Lemma G.24
.

For all
B_{r}
and
B_{c}
, we have

\displaystyle\|B_{r}-B_{r}^{*}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 6\sqrt{\frac{\delta_{B_{r}}}{m_{r}}}

(89)

\displaystyle\|B_{c}-B_{c}^{*}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 6\sqrt{\frac{\delta_{B_{c}}}{m_{c}}}

(90)

\displaystyle\|B_{r}-B_{r}^{*}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\text{ and }\|B_{c}-B_{c}^{*}\|_{op}\leq 2\kern 5.0pt.

(91)

The following lemma is a straightforward adaptation of Lemma
G.20
.

Lemma G.25
.

There exists a positive numerical constant
c
such that

\log\left[|\mathcal{B}_{r,j}|\right]\leq cj\left[\log(n)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}\right]\ ,

for any
j=1,\ldots,2n
. A similar result holds for
\mathcal{B}_{c,j}
.

Now, we are in position to apply Hanson-Wright inequality for Gaussian variables (e.g. Lemma 1
[
46
]
) to all
B_{r}
belonging to
\mathcal{B}_{r,j}
, this for all
j=1,\ldots,2n
. For a fixed
B_{r}
, the random variable
N_{2}(B_{r})
is of form
U^{T}HU
where
U
is a standard Gaussian vector of dimension
nK
, and
H
is a symmetric matrix satisfying
tr[H]=0
,
\|H\|_{F}\leq 6\sqrt{K\frac{\delta_{B_{r}}}{m_{r}}}
and
\|H\|_{op}\leq 2
. We deduce, that with probability
higher than
1/(n\vee p)^{2}
, we have

N_{2}(B_{r})\leq c\left[\delta_{B_{r}}\vee 1\right]\left[\sqrt{\frac{K}{m_{r}}[\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}]}+\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}\right].

for any
B_{r}\neq B_{r}^{*}
. A similar bound holds for
N_{1}(B_{c})
. For
N_{3}(B_{c},B_{r})
, we apply Hanson-Wright inequality to all
B_{r}
and
B_{c}
belonging to
\mathcal{B}_{c,j}
and
\mathcal{B}_{r,j^{\prime}}
. The random variable
N_{3}(B_{c},B_{r})
is of form
U^{T}HU
where
U
is a standard Gaussian vector of dimension
np
, and
H
is a symmetric matrix defined by; for
(i,j)\in[n]\times[p]
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in[n]\times[p]
, we have
H_{(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}=\left(B_{r}-B_{r}^{*}\right)_{ii^{\prime}}\left(B_{c}-B^{*}_{c}\right)_{jj^{\prime}}
. This matrix satisfies
tr[H]=0
,
\|H\|_{F}\leq 36\sqrt{\frac{\delta_{B_{r}}\delta_{B_{c}}}{m_{c}m_{r}}}
and
\|H\|_{op}\leq 4
. We deduce, that with probability
higher than
1/(n\vee p)^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle N_{3}(B_{c},B_{r})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\left[\sqrt{\frac{\delta_{B_{r}}\delta_{B_{c}}[\delta_{B_{r}}+\delta_{B_{c}}+1]}{m_{r}m_{c}}\left[\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle+c[\delta_{B_{r}}+\delta_{B_{c}}+1]\left[\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right]\ ,

as long as
B_{c}\neq B^{*}_{c}
and
B_{r}\neq B_{r}^{*}
. Recall that
\delta_{B_{r}}\leq 2n
and
\delta_{B_{c}}\leq 2p
. Besides, we know from Lemma 9 in
[
28
]
that
\delta_{B_{r}}\geq m_{r}/(m_{r}^{+})
if
B_{r}\neq B_{r}^{*}
and
\delta_{B_{c}}\geq m_{c}/(m_{c}^{+})
if
B_{c}\neq B_{c}^{*}
.
This leads to

\displaystyle N_{3}(B_{c},B_{r})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}\delta_{B_{r}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{p}{m_{r}m_{c}}\left[\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle+c\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\delta_{B_{c}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{n}{m_{r}m_{c}}\left[\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle+c\left[\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}\delta_{B_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\delta_{B_{c}}\right]\left[\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right]\ .

∎

For a fixed
B
,
C(B)
is distributed a Gaussian random variable whose variance is given by

4\|B_{r}XB_{c}-B^{*}_{r}XB^{*}_{c}\|_{F}^{2}=4\mathrm{Tr}\left[X^{T}X-X^{T}B_{r}XB_{c}\right]=4S(B_{r},B_{c})\ ,

since
X=B_{r}^{*}XB^{*}_{c}
. Then, we apply an union bound over all
\mathcal{B}_{r,j}
’s and all
\mathcal{B}_{c,j^{\prime}}
. Together with Lemma
G.25
, this allows us to conclude that, with probability higher than
1-c^{\prime}/(n\vee p)^{2}
, we have

C(B_{c},B_{r})\leq c\sqrt{S(B_{r},B_{r})\left[\lceil\delta_{B_{r}}\rceil\left(\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{r}^{+}}{m_{r}}\right)+\lceil\delta_{B_{c}}\rceil\left(\log(n\vee p)+\frac{m_{c}^{+}}{m_{c}}\right)\right]}\ .

∎

H
Proofs for technical discussions

It is a consequence of the following lemma whose proof is given below.

Lemma H.1
.

There exists a subset
\bar{J}\subseteq J^{*}
and a subset
\mathcal{K^{\prime}}\subset[K]
with
|\mathcal{K^{\prime}}|\geq\frac{9K}{10}
satisfying;

1.

For all
j\in\bar{J}
,
\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|\left(\mu_{k}\right)_{j}^{2}\geq\frac{n\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{80s\gamma}
;

2.

For all
k\neq l\in\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
,
\|(\mu_{k})_{\bar{J}}-(\mu_{l})_{\bar{J}}\|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}
.

Assume that there exist
k\in\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
and
l\in[K]
such that
\|(\mu_{k})_{\bar{J}}-(\mu_{l})_{\bar{J}}\|^{2}<\frac{1}{8}\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}
. By Lemma
H.1
, we deduce that for the all
k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K^{\prime}}\setminus\{k\}
, we have

\|(\mu_{k^{\prime}})_{\bar{J}}-(\mu_{l})_{\bar{J}}\|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{8}\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}
. As a consequence, there exist at most
K/10
elements
k\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
such that there exists
l\in[K]
with
\|(\mu_{k})_{\bar{J}}-(\mu_{l})_{\bar{J}}\|^{2}<\frac{1}{8}\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}
. Defining
\mathcal{K}
by removing all these elements from
\mathcal{K}
, we arrive at the desired conclusion.
∎

We define
\bar{J}
the set of all
j
such that
\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|\left(\mu_{k}\right)_{j}^{2}\geq\frac{n\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{80s\gamma}
. Since the signal is supported on
J^{*}
which is of size at most
s
, we deduce that
\sum_{j\notin\bar{J}}\sum_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|\left(\mu_{k}\right)_{j}^{2}\leq\frac{n\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{80\gamma}
. Let
\mathcal{K}^{-}
the set of all
k\in[K]
with
\|\left(\mu_{k}\right)_{J^{*}\setminus\bar{J}}\|^{2}_{2}\geq\frac{\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{8}
. We have

|\mathcal{K}^{-}|\frac{n}{K\gamma}\frac{\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{8}\leq\frac{n\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{80\gamma}\kern 5.0pt,

which, in turn, implies that
|\mathcal{K^{-}}|\leq\frac{K}{10}
.

We set
\mathcal{K^{\prime}}=[K]\setminus\mathcal{K}^{-}
which is of size at least
\frac{9K}{10}
. For
k,l\in\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
, we have

\|(\mu_{k})_{\bar{J}}-(\mu_{l})_{\bar{J}}\|^{2}\geq\left(\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|-2\frac{\Delta\sigma}{\sqrt{8}}\right)^{2}\geq\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\Delta\sigma\right)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Suppose that
X
satisfies Assumption
2
for some
\eta\geq 1
. Since
\min_{k\neq l}\frac{\|\mu_{k}-\mu_{l}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\geq\Delta^{2}
, we deduce that at least all except one of the
\mu_{k}
’s satisfy
\|\mu_{k}\|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}
. We deduce that

\sum_{j\in J^{*}}\|X_{:j}\|^{2}\geq(K-1)\min_{k\in[K]}|G_{k}^{*}|\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}\geq\frac{n(K-1)}{2K\gamma}\Delta^{2}\sigma^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

On the other hand,

\sum_{j\in J^{*}}\|X_{:j}\|^{2}\leq s\max_{j\in J^{*}}\|X_{:j}\|^{2}\leq s\eta w_{J^{*}}^{2}\sigma^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

We conclude the proof of the lemma with

w_{J^{*}}^{2}\geq\frac{n(K-1)}{2sK\gamma\eta}\Delta^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

We have

\displaystyle n(n-1)\ MMSE_{poly}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]-\sup_{\hat{M}\ poly-time,\ \operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|\hat{M}\|^{2}_{F}\right]=1}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle M^{*},\hat{M}\rangle_{F}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=:\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]-corr^{2}={n^{2}\over K}(1+o(1))-corr^{2}.

In particular
corr^{2}=o(n^{2}/K)
.
Since

\sup_{\hat{G}\ poly-time}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\langle M^{*},M^{\hat{G}}\rangle_{F}\right]\leq\sqrt{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{\hat{G}}\|^{2}_{F}\right]}\ corr

and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{\hat{G}}\|^{2}_{F}\right]\geq n^{2}/K
,
we get

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{G}\ poly-time}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{*}-M^{\hat{G}}\|^{2}_{F}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{\hat{G}}\|^{2}_{F}\right]-2\sqrt{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{\hat{G}}\|^{2}_{F}\right]}\ corr

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{*}\|^{2}_{F}\right]+\min_{a\geq n/\sqrt{K}}(a^{2}-2a\ corr)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|M^{*}\|^{2}_{K}\right]+{n^{2}\over K}-{2n\over\sqrt{K}}corr={2n^{2}\over K}(1+o(1))\kern 5.0pt,

where we used
corr^{2}=o(n^{2}/K)
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\|M^{*}\|^{2}_{F}]=n^{2}K^{-1}(1+o(1))
for the last two equalities.
∎

The proof is obtained by combining Lemma
A.2
with the following lemma.

Lemma H.2
.

Assume that both
G^{*}
and
G
are
\gamma
-balanced as defined in (
24
). Then, it
follows that

[1-err(G,G^{*})]^{2}\leq\gamma^{2}-\frac{K\|M^{G}-M^{*}\|_{F}^{2}}{2n^{2}}\ .

∎

Without loss of generality, we assume that the permutation
\pi
in the definition of
err(G,G^{*})
is the identity.

err(G,G^{*})=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G_{k}\Delta G^{*}_{k}|=1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G_{k}\cap G^{*}_{k}|\ ,

which implies that
\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G_{k}\cap G^{*}_{k}|=n[1-err(G,G^{*})]
.
Let us define
N_{1}(G)=|\{(i,j):i\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\sim}}j\}|
,

N_{1}(G^{*})=|\{(i,j):i\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G^{*}}}{{\sim}}j\}|
,
and
N_{11}=|\{(i,j):i\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G^{*}}}{{\sim}}j\text{ and }i\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G}}{{\sim}}j\}|
.
Expanding the squares, we have, for
\gamma
-balanced partitions
G,G^{*}
, that

\|M^{G}-M^{*}\|_{F}^{2}=2[N_{1}(G)+N_{1}(G^{*})]-4N_{11}\leq 2\gamma^{2}\frac{n^{2}}{K}-4N_{1,1}\ .

(92)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle N_{11}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\sum_{k}|G_{k}\cap G^{*}_{k}|^{2}

(93)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2K}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}|G_{k}\cap G^{*}_{k}|\right]^{2}=\frac{n^{2}}{2K}[1-err(G,G^{*})]^{2}\ ,

where we used Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the second line. Plugging (
93
) in (
92
)
gives

{K\over 2n^{2}}\|M^{G}-M^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\gamma^{2}-[1-err(G,G^{*})]^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of Lemma
H.2
is complete
∎

Given any subset
I\subset\{3,\ldots,n\}
and any
j=1,\ldots,p
, define the matrix
\alpha^{(I,j)}

\alpha^{(I,j)}_{i,j^{\prime}}=\mathbf{1}\{j^{\prime}=j\}\mathbf{1}\{i\in I\cup\{1,2\}\}

By permutation invariance of the problem
\kappa_{x,\alpha^{(I,j)}}
does not depend on
j
and only depends on
|I|
through its cardinality. Denote
I_{0}=\{3,\ldots,D\}
.
By permutation invariance, we know that

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{corr}^{(SW)}_{\leq D}\right)^{2}\geq p\binom{n-2}{D-2}\kappa^{2}_{x,\alpha^{(I_{0},1)}}\ .

(94)

To alleviate the notation, we henceforth write
\alpha
for
\alpha^{(I_{0},1)}
.

From Theorem
2.5
, we deduce that

\kappa_{x,\alpha}=\lambda^{D}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\alpha)}C_{x,\beta_{1}(\pi),\ldots,\beta_{l}(\pi)}\kern 5.0pt,

(95)

where
l=D/2
here. We recall that
\beta_{s}=\beta_{s}(\pi)
satisfies
|\beta_{s}(\pi)|=2
so that we can write

\beta_{s}
as
\left\{(i_{s},1),(i^{\prime}_{s},1)\right\}
. Equipped with this notation, we have

\displaystyle C_{x,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cum}\left(x,z_{1}\mathbf{1}_{k^{*}_{i_{1}}=k^{*}_{i^{\prime}_{1}}},\ldots,z_{1}\mathbf{1}_{k^{*}_{i_{l}}=k^{*}_{i^{\prime}_{l}}}\right),

(96)

In order to compute (
96
), we apply the law of total cumulance (Lemma
2.3
) by conditionning on
z_{1}
. Let us define
W_{0}:=x
and
W_{s}=z_{1}\mathbf{1}_{k^{*}_{i_{s}}=k^{*}_{i^{\prime}_{s}}}
for
s\in[l]
.
Consider any partition
\overline{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}([0;l])
. By Lemma
2.3
, we have

C_{x,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l}}=\sum_{\overline{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}([0;l])}\mathrm{Cum}\left(\mathrm{Cum}\left((W_{i})_{i\in R}|z_{1}\right)_{R\in\overline{\pi}}\right)

Denote
R_{0}
the group that contains
W_{0}=x
. If
|R_{0}|=1
, then
\mathrm{Cum}\left(W_{0}|z_{1}\right)=1/K
and is constant almost surely. As a consequence, we have
\mathrm{Cum}\left(\mathrm{Cum}\left((W_{i})_{i\in R}|z_{1}\right)_{R\in\overline{\pi}}\right)=0
by Lemma
B.2
since a constant is independent from any other random variable. If
|R_{0}|=2
and the other random variable
W_{s}\in R_{0}
is of the form
z_{1}\mathbf{1}_{k^{*}_{1}=k^{*}_{2}}
, we have
\mathrm{Cum}\left((W_{i})_{i\in R_{0}}|z_{1}\right)=z_{1}K^{-1}(1-1/K)
. For any other choice of
R_{0}
, we claim that
\mathrm{Cum}\left((W_{i})_{i\in R}|z_{1}\right)=0
. Indeed, conditionally to
z_{1}
,
\mathbf{1}_{k^{*}_{1}=k^{*}_{2}}
is independent from all the other random variables since each
k^{*}_{i}
, for
i\in[D]
occurs at most once in the other random variables. Now consider a group
R\neq R_{0}
of
\overline{\pi}
that do not contain
0
. For the same independence argument, we have
\mathrm{Cum}\left((W_{i})_{i\in R}|z_{1}\right)=0
if
|R|>1
. We conclude that
C_{x,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l}}=0
unless there exists
s\in[l]
such that
\beta_{s}=(1,2)
, in which case, we have

C_{x,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{l}}=\frac{1}{K^{l}}\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right)\mathrm{Cum}\left(z_{1},\ldots,z_{1}\right)

Coming back to (
95
) and counting , we conclude that

\kappa_{x,\alpha}=\frac{(D-2)!}{2^{D/2-1}(D/2-1)!}\cdot\frac{1}{K^{D/2}}\lambda^{D}\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right)\mathrm{Cum}\left(z_{1},\ldots,z_{1}\right)

Let us lower bound the cumulant
\mathrm{Cum}\left(z_{1},\ldots,z_{1}\right)
between Bernoulli distribution of parameter
\rho
. By Möbius formula in Lemma
B.1
, we have

\mathrm{Cum}\left(z_{1},\ldots,z_{1}\right)\geq\rho-\rho^{2}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}(l)}(|\pi|-1)!\geq\rho-6\rho^{2}l!2^{l}\ ,

where we used the same computation as in the proof of Corollary
2.6
. Coming back to (
94
) and relying on our condition, we conclude that

\left(\widetilde{corr}^{(SW)}_{\leq D}\right)^{2}\geq c^{\prime}e^{-cD\log(D)}pn^{D-2}\frac{1}{K^{D}}\lambda^{2D}\rho^{2}\ ,

where
c
and
c^{\prime}
are positive numerical constants.

∎
